=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two strings word1 and word2.
A string x is called valid if x can be rearranged to have word2 as a prefix.
Return the total number of valid substrings of word1.
 
Example 1:

Input: word1 = "bcca", word2 = "abc"
Output: 1
Explanation:
The only valid substring is "bcca" which can be rearranged to "abcc" having "abc" as a prefix.

Example 2:

Input: word1 = "abcabc", word2 = "abc"
Output: 10
Explanation:
All the substrings except substrings of size 1 and size 2 are valid.

Example 3:

Input: word1 = "abcabc", word2 = "aaabc"
Output: 0

 
Constraints:

1 <= word1.length <= 10^5
1 <= word2.length <= 10^4
word1 and word2 consist only of lowercase English letters.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def validSubstringCount(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's consider using bytes for word1 to avoid ord repeated:
def validSubstringCount(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> int:
    target = [0]*26
    for ch in word2:
        target[ord(ch)-97] += 1
    missing = sum(1 for v in target if v > 0)
    cnt = [0]*26
    n = len(word1)
    r = 0
    ans = 0
    for l, ch in enumerate(word1):
        while r < n and missing:
            c = ord(word1[r]) - 97
            cnt[c] += 1
            if cnt[c] == target[c]:
                missing -= 1
            r += 1
        if missing == 0:
            ans += n - r + 1
        else:
            break
        c = ord(ch) - 97
        if cnt[c] == target[c]:
            missing += 1
        cnt[c] -= 1
    return ans

Potential issue: word1 and word2 consist lowercase English letters; using ord(ch)-97 okay. Could precompute arr = [ord(c)-97 for c in word1] for faster and to avoid repeated ord in while and removal. Let's do that in final code for clarity/efficiency:
class Solution:
    def validSubstringCount(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> int:
        target = [0] * 26
        for ch in word2:
            target[ord(ch) - 97] += 1
        missing = sum(1 for v in target if v > 0)
        cnt = [0] * 26
        n = len(word1)
        nums = [ord(c) - 97 for c in word1]
        r = 0
        ans = 0
        for l in range(n):
            while r < n and missing > 0:
                c = nums[r]
                cnt[c] += 1
                if cnt[c] == target[c]:
                    missing -= 1
                r += 1
            if missing == 0:
                ans += n - r + 1
            else:
                break
            c = nums[l]
            if cnt[c] == target[c]:
                missing += 1
            cnt[c] -= 1
        return ans

Suppose target[c]=2, cnt old 1, add ->2, missing--. Good. Suppose target[c]=1, cnt old 1, add ->2, cnt==target? 2==1 false. missing already 0 for c. Good. No negative. No. If cnt[c] < target[c] before removal, missing already includes c; no change. If cnt[c] > target[c], missing doesn't include c; no change. If cnt[c] == target[c], missing doesn't include c; after removal includes. Good.

Let's ensure final code is syntactically correct:
class Solution:
    def validSubstringCount(self, word1: str, word2: str) -> int:
        target = [0] * 26
        for ch in word2:
            target[ord(ch) - ord('a')] += 1

        missing = sum(1 for v in target if v > 0)
        cnt = [0] * 26
        n = len(word1)
        nums = [ord(ch) - ord('a') for ch in word1]

        r = 0
        ans = 0
        for l in range(n):
            while r < n and missing > 0:
                c = nums[r]
                cnt[c] += 1
                if cnt[c] == target[c]:
                    missing -= 1
                r += 1

            if missing == 0:
                ans += n - r + 1
            else:
                break

            c = nums[l]
            if cnt[c] == target[c]:
                missing += 1
            cnt[c] -= 1

        return ans

Let's maybe think about proof of monotonicity for final explanation if needed:
- For a fixed left index l, if [l, r] is valid, then [l, r'] for r' >= r is valid because adding chars cannot reduce counts.
- Therefore valid right endpoints for l form a suffix [min_r, n-1].
- As l increases, min_r never decreases because [l+1, r] has counts <= [l, r].
- Use two pointers.
